It’s been frustrating, and I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're not paying attention when your wife is talking, or do you feel like you are paying attention but just can't seem to remember what she said later?\n\nPatient: I feel like I am paying attention at the time. But after a while my mind drifts, and then I’m looking back over the same notes like I never really read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: How much do these memory lapses and difficulties with focusing affect your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: It affects things some, yeah. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but it has started causing tension at home. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t count on me as much with my cousin’s care.\n\nAnd with catering stuff, I’ve had to go back and double-check things or redo small tasks so orders don’t get mixed up. So it’s manageable, but it’s definitely causing problems.\n\nDoctor: How distressed or upset do you feel about these memory lapses and difficulties with focusing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m extremely upset by it, but it does bother me. Mostly I feel frustrated or embarrassed when I realize I missed something important, especially if my wife has to repeat herself. It’s more of a steady annoyance and worry that I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or hopeless about your situation, or have you been worrying excessively about things beyond your memory lapses?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I get frustrated about the memory and focus stuff, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless or empty. And I don’t think I’m worrying a lot beyond that. It’s mostly just the annoyance around forgetting things and trying not to let people down.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in how you interact with friends or other family members because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Mostly at home, yeah. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t really count on me the same way with my cousin’s care, and that causes some tension.\n\nWith other family or friends, I haven’t noticed as much, but the home stuff is definitely where it shows up. And with catering tasks, I’ve had to double-check or redo little things so nothing gets mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your work or ability to perform your job due to these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really in a regular job right now, but with the catering tasks I help with, yeah, it has shown up a bit. I’ve had to double-check things or redo small jobs so orders or details don’t get mixed up. It’s not a huge problem, but it slows me down and makes me feel like I have to be more careful than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your cognitive abilities compared to how you used to function a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been a major decline over years, but it has been noticeably harder for about the last 11 months. That’s around when my cousin moved in with us after surgery, and since then I’ve been more aware that I’m not keeping up as easily as I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant physical health changes or illnesses around the same time you started noticing these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t think of any major physical illness or health change that happened around then. Nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or emotionally drained by your daily responsibilities and interactions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say overwhelmed, exactly. Some days I do feel worn out by keeping up with things or dealing with people, but it’s not constant or extreme. It’s more like I get tired and need a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with learning new information or skills?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might need a little more time with something new than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a major problem learning new information or skills.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t really count on me the same way with my cousin’s care, and that causes some tension.\n\nWith other family or friends, I haven’t noticed as much, but the home stuff is definitely where it shows up. And with catering tasks, I’ve had to double-check or redo little things so nothing gets mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your work or ability to perform your job due to these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really in a regular job right now, but with the catering tasks I help with, yeah, it has shown up a bit. I’ve had to double-check things or redo small jobs so orders or details don’t get mixed up. It’s not a huge problem, but it slows me down and makes me feel like I have to be more careful than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your cognitive abilities compared to how you used to function a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been a major decline over years, but it has been noticeably harder for about the last 11 months. That’s around when my cousin moved in with us after surgery, and since then I’ve been more aware that I’m not keeping up as easily as I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant physical health changes or illnesses around the same time you started noticing these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t think of any major physical illness or health change that happened around then. Nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or emotionally drained by your daily responsibilities and interactions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say overwhelmed, exactly. Some days I do feel worn out by keeping up with things or dealing with people, but it’s not constant or extreme. It’s more like I get tired and need a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with learning new information or skills?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might need a little more time with something new than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a major problem learning new information or skills.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your difficulties with memory and focus are interfering with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. It’s not like I can’t function, but it has started causing problems. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t always count on me with helping care for my cousin. And with the catering stuff, I’ve had to double-check things or redo small tasks so they don’t get mixed up. So, yeah, it does get in the way day to day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.8, F88\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what really made me come in is… my wife will be telling me things, like about my cousin’s appointments or rides, and I’ll be looking right at her like I’m listening. But then a minute later I realize I didn’t really take in half of what she said. It’s been frustrating, and I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're not paying attention when your wife is talking, or do you feel like you are paying attention but just can't seem to remember what she said later?\n\nPatient: I feel like I am paying attention at the time. I’ll be looking right at her while she’s telling me about my cousin’s appointments or rides, but then a minute later I realize I didn’t really take in a lot of what she said.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only when your wife is talking to you, or do you also find yourself forgetting things you've read or other information you've tried to remember?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not just with my wife. If I sit down to work through something like a catering order, or I’m on a parish planning call, I’ll start out following it fine. But after a while my mind drifts, and then I’m looking back over the same notes like I never really read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: How much do these memory lapses and difficulties with focusing affect your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: It affects things some, yeah. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but it has started causing tension at home. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t count on me as much with my cousin’s care.\n\nAnd with catering stuff, I’ve had to go back and double-check things or redo small tasks so orders don’t get mixed up. So it’s manageable, but it’s definitely causing problems.\n\nDoctor: How distressed or upset do you feel about these memory lapses and difficulties with focusing?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m extremely upset by it, but it does bother me. Mostly I feel frustrated or embarrassed when I realize I missed something important, especially if my wife has to repeat herself. It’s more of a steady annoyance and worry that I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or hopeless about your situation, or have you been worrying excessively about things beyond your memory lapses?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I get frustrated about the memory and focus stuff, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless or empty. And I don’t think I’m worrying a lot beyond that. It’s mostly just the annoyance around forgetting things and trying not to let people down.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in how you interact with friends or other family members because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Mostly at home, yeah. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t really count on me the same way with my cousin’s care, and that causes some tension.\n\nWith other family or friends, I haven’t noticed as much, but the home stuff is definitely where it shows up. And with catering tasks, I’ve had to double-check or redo little things so nothing gets mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your work or ability to perform your job due to these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really in a regular job right now, but with the catering tasks I help with, yeah, it has shown up a bit. I’ve had to double-check things or redo small jobs so orders or details don’t get mixed up. It’s not a huge problem, but it slows me down and makes me feel like I have to be more careful than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your cognitive abilities compared to how you used to function a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been a major decline over years, but it has been noticeably harder for about the last 11 months. That’s around when my cousin moved in with us after surgery, and since then I’ve been more aware that I’m not keeping up as easily as I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant physical health changes or illnesses around the same time you started noticing these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t think of any major physical illness or health change that happened around then. Nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or emotionally drained by your daily responsibilities and interactions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say overwhelmed, exactly. Some days I do feel worn out by keeping up with things or dealing with people, but it’s not constant or extreme. It’s more like I get tired and need a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with learning new information or skills?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might need a little more time with something new than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a major problem learning new information or skills.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your difficulties with memory and focus are interfering with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. And with the catering stuff, I’ve had to double-check things or redo small tasks so they don’t get mixed up. It seemed to start around when my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. Since then, the memory lapses and focusing have just been more of an issue.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your frustration or annoyance about these memory and focus issues as feeling emotionally distressing to you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s emotionally distressing, exactly. It’s been frustrating, and I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're not paying attention when your wife is talking, or do you feel like you are paying attention but just can't seem to remember what she said later?\n\nPatient: I feel like I am paying attention at the time. I’ll be looking right at her while she’s telling me about my cousin’s appointments or rides, but then a minute later I realize I didn’t really take in a lot of what she said.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen only when your wife is talking to you, or do you also find yourself forgetting things you've read or other information you've tried to remember?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not just with my wife. If I sit down to work through something like a catering order, or I’m on a parish planning call, I’ll start out following it fine. But after a while my mind drifts, and then I’m looking back over the same notes like I never really read them in the first place.\n\nDoctor: How much do these memory lapses and difficulties with focusing affect your daily life and relationships?\n\nPatient: It affects things some, yeah. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but it has started causing tension at home. Mostly I feel frustrated or embarrassed when I realize I missed something important, especially if my wife has to repeat herself. It’s more of a steady annoyance and worry that I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or hopeless about your situation, or have you been worrying excessively about things beyond your memory lapses?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I get frustrated about the memory and focus stuff, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless or empty. And I don’t think I’m worrying a lot beyond that. It’s mostly just the annoyance around forgetting things and trying not to let people down.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in how you interact with friends or other family members because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Mostly at home, yeah. It’s more of a steady annoyance and worry that I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or hopeless about your situation, or have you been worrying excessively about things beyond your memory lapses?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I get frustrated about the memory and focus stuff, but I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless or empty. And I don’t think I’m worrying a lot beyond that. It’s mostly just the annoyance around forgetting things and trying not to let people down.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in how you interact with friends or other family members because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Mostly at home, yeah. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t really count on me the same way with my cousin’s care, and that causes some tension.\n\nWith other family or friends, I haven’t noticed as much, but the home stuff is definitely where it shows up. And with catering tasks, I’ve had to double-check or redo little things so nothing gets mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your work or ability to perform your job due to these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really in a regular job right now, but with the catering tasks I help with, yeah, it has shown up a bit. I’ve had to double-check things or redo small jobs so orders or details don’t get mixed up. It’s not a huge problem, but it slows me down and makes me feel like I have to be more careful than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your cognitive abilities compared to how you used to function a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been a major decline over years, but it has been noticeably harder for about the last 11 months. That’s around when my cousin moved in with us after surgery, and since then I’ve been more aware that I’m not keeping up as easily as I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant physical health changes or illnesses around the same time you started noticing these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t think of any major physical illness or health change that happened around then. Nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or emotionally drained by your daily responsibilities and interactions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say overwhelmed, exactly. Some days I do feel worn out by keeping up with things or dealing with people, but it’s not constant or extreme. It’s more like I get tired and need a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with learning new information or skills?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might need a little more time with something new than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a major problem learning new information or skills.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your difficulties with memory and focus are interfering with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. It’s not like I can’t function, but it has started causing problems. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t always count on me with helping care for my cousin. And with the catering stuff, I’ve had to double-check things or redo small tasks so they don’t get mixed up. So, yeah, it does get in the way day to day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your symptoms of memory lapses and difficulties with focusing seemed to worsen or improve significantly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been noticeably harder for about 11 months now. It seemed to start around when my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. Since then, the memory lapses and focusing have just been more of an issue.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your frustration or annoyance about these memory and focus issues as feeling emotionally distressing to you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s emotionally distressing, exactly. It’s frustrating and annoying when it happens, but I don’t feel overwhelmed by it or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your frustration with these memory and focus issues is affecting your mood or overall sense of well-being?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I get irritated in the moment when I forget something or lose track, but I wouldn’t say it’s changing my overall mood or how I feel about life day to day. It’s more of a nuisance than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and focus issues are causing you significant emotional pain or distress?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d call it significant emotional pain. It’s annoying, sure, and I get frustrated when it happens, but it doesn’t feel deeply distressing to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s mostly just the annoyance around forgetting things and trying not to let people down.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any changes in how you interact with friends or other family members because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Mostly at home, yeah. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t really count on me the same way with my cousin’s care, and that causes some tension.\n\nWith other family or friends, I haven’t noticed as much, but the home stuff is definitely where it shows up. And with catering tasks, I’ve had to double-check or redo little things so nothing gets mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your work or ability to perform your job due to these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: I’m not really in a regular job right now, but with the catering tasks I help with, yeah, it has shown up a bit. I’ve had to double-check things or redo small jobs so orders or details don’t get mixed up. It’s not a huge problem, but it slows me down and makes me feel like I have to be more careful than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your cognitive abilities compared to how you used to function a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been a major decline over years, but it has been noticeably harder for about the last 11 months. That’s around when my cousin moved in with us after surgery, and since then I’ve been more aware that I’m not keeping up as easily as I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant physical health changes or illnesses around the same time you started noticing these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t think of any major physical illness or health change that happened around then. Nothing really stands out to me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel overwhelmed or emotionally drained by your daily responsibilities and interactions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say overwhelmed, exactly. Some days I do feel worn out by keeping up with things or dealing with people, but it’s not constant or extreme. It’s more like I get tired and need a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with learning new information or skills?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might need a little more time with something new than I used to, but I haven’t noticed a major problem learning new information or skills.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your difficulties with memory and focus are interfering with your ability to complete tasks or achieve your goals on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. It’s not like I can’t function, but it has started causing problems. My wife gets frustrated because she feels like she can’t always count on me with helping care for my cousin. And with the catering stuff, I’ve had to double-check things or redo small tasks so they don’t get mixed up. So, yeah, it does get in the way day to day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your symptoms of memory lapses and difficulties with focusing seemed to worsen or improve significantly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been noticeably harder for about 11 months now. It seemed to start around when my cousin moved in with us after his surgery. Since then, the memory lapses and focusing have just been more of an issue.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your frustration or annoyance about these memory and focus issues as feeling emotionally distressing to you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s emotionally distressing, exactly. It’s frustrating and annoying when it happens, but I don’t feel overwhelmed by it or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your frustration with these memory and focus issues is affecting your mood or overall sense of well-being?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I get irritated in the moment when I forget something or lose track, but I wouldn’t say it’s changing my overall mood or how I feel about life day to day. It’s more of a nuisance than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and focus issues are causing you significant emotional pain or distress?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d call it significant emotional pain. It’s annoying, sure, and I get frustrated when it happens, but it doesn’t feel deeply distressing to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these memory and focus issues are significantly interfering with your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: It’s not ruining my life or anything, but it does interfere some. It’s caused some tension at home because my wife feels like she can’t always count on me with my cousin’s care. And with catering stuff, I have to double-check myself or redo little things so jobs don’t get mixed up. So yeah, it affects things, just not in a huge way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your memory and focus issues are causing you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: No, not a lot. I mean, it gets under my skin sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it causes me real emotional distress. It’s more frustrating in the moment than anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
